- **"Three places project onto the line exactly where Zentsū-ji does" — *exactly* is literal.**
  All four project to the identical along-line value, **63.80976512540966 km**, at 102.5, 283.1,
  295.9 and 102.8 m off. Three of the four are within 20 m of the 300 m limit and none is
  dropped ✓. This is the best-grounded sentence in the six.
- **The Temple 72 → 73 inversion is handled correctly.** `temple-73` projects at **59.678** and
  `temple-72` at **60.135** — the line does run the numbering backwards, and this is the only
  such pair in the pilgrimage. The sentence makes **no order claim**: the verb "come" governs
  "Five more temples", the pair is an appositive, and its only predicate — "0.46 km apart" — is
  symmetric. Two things settle it in the drafter's favour. First, the five temples are *not*
  listed in sequence in that sentence; only two of the five are named, and they are picked out as
  a pair rather than presented as a stretch of the walk, which is what weakens the ordering
  implicature the rest of the narrative would otherwise carry. Second, **the data establishes no
  order to assert**: both coordinates are rounded to three decimals — `temple-72` `[133.75,
  34.223]`, `temple-73` `[133.75, 34.219]`, verified in `waypoints.geojson`, about 445 m apart
  and differing only in latitude — so a text asserting *either* order would be claiming more than
  the files hold. A sentence that asserts none is the only correct one available, and the 0.46 km
  it does assert is declared in this section's own `description`. Keeping the pair is right;
  striking it would delete a declared figure to buy nothing.
- **The section-local superlative is right for the same reason 911 m is.** "the narrowest step
  between two temples this section records" — 0.117 km is also the narrowest measured on any of
  the four lines (Iyo 0.882, Awa 1.111, Tosa 3.849), and stating that would attach a fact to the
  corpus rather than to the day. Consistent with the ruling on stage 0.
- **Describing the three co-projected nodes by `subtype` is reticence and it is correct
  reticence.** 五智院 and 延命地蔵尊 would each need romanising or translating, and
  善通寺宿坊いろは会館 carries 宿坊 — temple lodging — in a name whose `subtype` says only
  `hostel`. Calling it "a place to stay" refuses to upgrade a name into a classification. Noted,
  not struck.

**Edited in Task 8c.** Seventeen figures to ten — Tosa's own worst, and this day holds nine temples, which
is more than any other of the forty.

```
theme:      Nine temples, and a hundred and seventeen metres between the first two
narrative:  More temples stand on this day than on any other of the six. It begins on a pair: Jinne-in 0.8 km in and Kannon-ji 117 m after it, the narrowest step between temples in this section. Motoyama-ji follows, and then the spacing opens — 11.7 km to Iyadani-ji, with 6.6 km inside it holding nothing mapped at all, ending at Mitoyo. Five more temples come in the 11.9 km after Iyadani-ji, two of them, Mandara-ji and Shusshaka-ji, 0.46 km apart. Three more places meet the way at exactly the point Zentsū-ji does — a sacred place, a wayside shrine and a place to stay — and Konzō-ji closes the day.
reflection: The closest two temples in this section stand on this day, and so does its emptiest stretch. Does a day have one shape, or as many as you notice?
```

- **"Three places project onto the line exactly where Zentsū-ji does" → "Three more places meet the way at
  exactly the point Zentsū-ji does".** *Project onto the line* is the pipeline's own operation named in the
  pipeline's own words, and Task 7a called the sentence "the best-grounded in the six" because *exactly* is
  literal: all four project to the identical value, 63.80976512540966 km. **The literalness is what had to
  survive, and the trap is what it could have become.** "Three places stand exactly where Zentsū-ji does"
  was drafted and **rejected**: the three stand 283, 296 and 103 m off the line, so a claim that they are in
  the same *place* is a claim about the ground that the files do not hold, and it would be the one edit in
  the twenty that turns a fact about a projection into a fact about the world. *Meet the way at exactly the
  point* is the projection in walker's terms — the point on the way nearest each of them — and it is where a
  walker would leave the way for any of the three. **"the way" is Task 8a's replacement for "the walked
  line" and is the vaguer of the two**, therefore not the stronger.
- **"the narrowest step between two temples this section records" → "the narrowest step between temples in
  this section".** *Records* goes and is **not** replaced, because the temples here are an exhaustively
  known set — 23 `templeNumber` features from the official-site source `metadata.json` declares — which is
  the same reason Awa says "the widest step between two temples in Awa" flat and Tosa says "No temple has
  stood on the way for 61.7 km" flat. **Task 7a's argued point was the section bound, not the hedge**, and
  "in this section" carries it word for word: 0.117 km is also the narrowest measured on any of the four
  lines and the sentence still does not say so.
- **"with 6.6 km inside it holding nothing marked at all" → "… holding nothing mapped at all".** One word.
- **"Nine of Sanuki's twenty-three temples stand on this day, more than on any other of the six" → "More
  temples stand on this day than on any other of the six"**, and **"Two of them come first and come
  together" → "It begins on a pair"**. Three figures out; the nine is the theme's own first word and the
  pair is named in the same breath. The comparison Task 7a verified — 2, 9, 2, 7, 3, 0 counting each day's
  own start out — is unchanged.
- **"Konzō-ji closes the day at 29.3 km" → "Konzō-ji closes the day"**, and **"Seventy-five places in all,
  seventeen of them places to eat" — cut.** The day's own distance, which every consumer prints beside the
  text, and an inventory. **This is where the stage did not come down further and the reason is recorded**:
  what is left is nine temples, and nine temples on one day cannot be given without the spacing that makes
  them a day rather than a list. The 11.7 km gap, the 6.6 km blank inside it, the 11.9 km holding five, and
  the 0.117 km and 0.46 km pairs are the whole of the day's shape, and each is a figure a walker feels.
- **"two of them, Mandara-ji and Shusshaka-ji, 0.46 km apart" is carried word for word.** It is the clause
  Task 7a spent the longest on: the pair is named in numbering order, the line puts them in the other order,
  and the only predicate — *0.46 km apart* — is symmetric, so the sentence asserts no order. Rewording it
  would have risked reintroducing one and would have detached the argument that keeps it.
- **Reflection: "The closest two temples this section records stand on this day" → "The closest two temples
  in this section stand on this day".** One word, matching the narrative's own new phrasing, on the same
  reasoning: the temples are a known set and the section bound is what the sentence claims.

**Read in Task 9b. The edit stands and the flag is cleared, and one verb is recorded.**

**The concern.** "Three places **project onto the line** exactly where Zentsū-ji does" became "Three more
places **meet the way** at exactly the point Zentsū-ji does". Task 7a called the drafted sentence "the
best-grounded in the six" because *exactly* is literal, and re-measured here it still is: `temple-75`,
`node/7530622665` 五智院, `node/9497498117` 延命地蔵尊 and `node/9491429062` 善通寺宿坊いろは会館 all project
to **63.80976512540966 km**, identical to fourteen decimal places. But they stand **102.5**, **283.1**,
**295.9** and **102.8 m** off that line, and *meet* is a contact verb where *project* was a geometric one. A
place 296 m from the route does not meet the route.

**Why it stands.** The predicate is a relation among four projections rather than a position: what the
sentence claims is that these four share one point of the way, which is exactly true and is exactly what a
walker can act on — one departure from the way serves all four. All four are off the line, Zentsū-ji
included at 102.5 m, so the sentence does not quietly promote three distant things into the company of a
near one. **Task 8c drafted and rejected the form that would have been the failure** — "Three places stand
exactly where Zentsū-ji does" — which is the claim about the ground the files do not hold, and rejecting it
is the right call recorded in the right place. And *the line* is on the vocabulary out-list, so restoring
the drafted verb would undo a decision made across all forty stages for a case whose stronger reading the
sentence's own qualifier, "at exactly the point", already resists. Recorded rather than acted on.

- **"the narrowest step between temples in this section" drops a hedge it does not owe.** The temples here
  are an exhaustively known set — 23 `templeNumber` features from the official-site source `metadata.json`
  declares — which is the ground on which Task 9a cleared Tosa's flat "No temple has stood on the way for
  61.7 km". **Task 7a's argued point on this clause was the section bound, not the hedge**, and "in this
  section" carries it word for word: 0.117 km is also the narrowest step measured on any of the four lines,
  and the sentence still does not say so. The reflection's matching change reads the same way.
- **"two of them, Mandara-ji and Shusshaka-ji, 0.46 km apart" is carried word for word**, so the clause Task
  7a spent longest on — the pair named in numbering order, the line running it the other way, and a
  symmetric predicate that asserts no order — still attaches to the words that argument was written about.
  Rewording it would have risked reintroducing an order the data does not establish.
- **"More temples stand on this day than on any other of the six" is a comparison out of one file** and
  needs no hedge; the per-day counts Task 7a verified, 2, 9, 2, 7, 3 and 0, are unchanged.
- **"holding nothing mapped at all" keeps the hedge inside the clause**, and 6.648 km is unchanged.

  other day here, thirty of them places to eat".** Task 7a accepted this sentence because "*filed* names the
  record and *here* bounds the comparison to Sanuki's own six". **The bound survives word for word** — *here*
  is untouched — and *filed* goes without replacement on Awa's stage 3 rule, because a per-kilometre
  comparison of six days counted the same way out of one file **is not an absence claim**: 5.72/km against
  2.73, 2.56, 1.43, 0.78 and 0.18, and a reader who takes it as "this day is the most crowded of the six" is
  misled by nothing. The 85 goes as inventory; the thirty places to eat stay, because a walker can act on
  them.
- **"Four of the six towns Sanuki records stand on this one day" → "Four of Sanuki's mapped towns stand on
  this one day".** *Records* to *mapped*, and the total out. The hedge is owed here — an unmapped town would
  still be a town — and *mapped* carries it.
- **"where the day ends at 14.9 km" → "where the day ends"**, and **"Two temples come among them, Dōryū-ji

  a count of two things named in the same clause.
- **"Sanuki Kokubun-ji is 0.8 km short of the band and Shiromine-ji 0.9 km past it" → "Sanuki Kokubun-ji
  falls just short of the band and Shiromine-ji just past it".** Two figures traded for the word *just*,
  which names a range containing both measurements — 0.791 km and 0.908 km — and is therefore weaker, not

```
theme:      The longest day of the forty, and the most crowded
narrative:  59.6 km, the longest day of the forty this cut made, and it is long for an absence: from 63.8 km to 149.9 km — 86.1 km — this section's record marks no place to stay at all, and this day is 59.6 of that stretch. No town falls in it either, until the one it ends at. What the day is not is empty. A hundred and sixty-three of Sanuki's three hundred and eighty-nine recorded places stand on it, nearly twice the next day's count, and the longest run with nothing marked on it is only 4.2 km. Seven temples come among them — Tennō-ji, Sanuki Kokubun-ji, Shiromine-ji, Negoro-ji, Ichinomiya-ji, Yashima-ji and Yakuri-ji — with forty-two bus stops, thirty-one places to eat and sixteen convenience stores. Everything except somewhere to stop.
reflection: The busiest day in Sanuki and the longest of the forty, and those are one fact: nothing recorded on it is a place to sleep. How much of what you carry is decided by what someone wrote down?
```

**Reviewed in Task 7a. One clause struck for ambiguity; the rest stands.**

Re-measured: span **59.61056**, `snap`, the longest of the forty ✓ (checked against all four
sections' 40 declared distances, and against this stage's own committed warning). The section's
nine `accommodation` features project at 5.803, 38.287, 43.590, 52.765, 60.214, **63.810**,
**149.864**, 150.140, 162.619 — **86.054 km** with none between, and this day's 82.479–142.090
lies wholly inside it ✓. Exactly one of the 163 features is a `town`, at the day's end ✓. Longest
run with nothing marked **inside the day** is **4.241 km** (94.284 → 98.526), next 4.088 ✓ — and
it is unchanged if the two dropped nodes (304 m at 93.265, 307 m at 134.491) are excluded. Seven
temples ✓, and of the 163: **42** `bus_stop`, **31** `food` (28 + 3), **16** `convenience_store` ✓.

- **Struck: ", nearly twice the next day's count".** The comparison the drafter meant is exact —
  163 against stage 2's 85, the runner-up, is 1.92. But "the next day" in a stage package a
  walker reads *on stage 3* means stage 4, and stage 4 carries **29** places: 163 / 29 = 5.6, so
  on the reading the words most naturally invite the sentence is false. "Count" as the head noun
  does not rescue it — "the next day's count" still reads temporally, and stage 2, the day it
  actually means, is the day *before*. A shorter true line beats an ambiguous one: the sentence
  now reads "A hundred and sixty-three of Sanuki's three hundred and eighty-nine recorded places
  stand on it, and the longest run with nothing marked on it is only 4.2 km." 163 of 389 on one of
  six days already carries the crowding, and the 4.2 km makes it concrete.
- **"this section's record" carries the qualifier.** The drafter asked whether it carries as much
  as the warning's "OpenStreetMap records". It carries enough: *record* is the operative noun, and
  the same paragraph says "recorded places" and "nothing marked on it", while the reflection ends
  on "what someone wrote down" — which is the plainest statement of the caveat anywhere in these
  eleven. The committed warning, which a walker sees beside the narrative, names OpenStreetMap
  outright. Accept.
- **"Everything except somewhere to stop" stands.** It is a reading of the counts, and every count
  under it is verified: 163 places, 42 bus stops, 31 places to eat, 16 shops, one town at the end,
  no accommodation anywhere on the day. The reading is that a stretch can be crowded and still have
  no bed, which is precisely what the figures show.
- **The reflection's inference is sound.** "the busiest day in Sanuki and the longest of the forty
  … are one fact: nothing recorded on it is a place to sleep" — the day is 59.611 km *because* the
  fallback arm found no named accommodation or town for 59.611 km (its own `end.note` says so),
  and it is the busiest by a different count entirely. The common cause is real and is stated by
  the files, not inferred from the two figures alone.
- **Four temple elevations stand on this day (250, 330, 280, 200) and none is cited** ✓, nor is the
  `viewpoint` at 130.089. Correct: four heights strung along a 59.6 km day is exactly the shape
  that would read as a profile.

**Edited in Task 8c.** Thirteen figures to eight, and the brief's own example of the register — a hundred
and sixty-three of three hundred and eighty-nine — is the first thing out.

More places stand on this one day than on any other of the six, and the longest run with nothing mapped on it is only 4.2 km. Seven temples come among them — Tennō-ji, Sanuki Kokubun-ji, Shiromine-ji, Negoro-ji, Ichinomiya-ji, Yashima-ji and Yakuri-ji — and thirty-one places to eat. Everything except somewhere to stop.

- **The machinery sentence is carried word for word.** Task 7a called it a faithful restatement of the

```
theme:      From the eighty-eighth to the first
narrative:  A temple stands at each end of this day and none between them: Ōkubo-ji, the eighty-eighth, and Ryōzen-ji, the first. 39.8 km, the second-longest of Sanuki's six days, and seven places recorded on it, the fewest of the six. Minshuku Yasokubo comes 0.2 km in and is the only place to stay, and the only named accommodation or town, the record holds anywhere past Ōkubo-ji. After it 24.7 km carry nothing marked at all, the longest such stretch in this section. Then a station, a shrine and a shop inside 1.1 km, then 12.6 km more with a single vending machine on them. The last thing the record marks is a bus stop 76 m short of the end, named for the temple it stands in front of. Ryōzen-ji closes Sanuki at 202.2 km, and Awa's first day starts from the same point.
reflection: Every other section ends where the next one begins; this one ends where the first one began. Who decides when a walk is finished?
```

**Reviewed in Task 7a — read hardest, as the drafter asked. Stands unchanged, and the one liberty
is allowed.**

Every figure re-measured on the committed line. Span vertex 7126 → vertex 9019 = **39.80182 km**,
`snap`, second-longest of the six (59.611 / **39.802** / 38.287 / 29.331 / 20.330 / 14.873) ✓.
**Seven** features carry `stageIndex: 5`, the fewest of 30 / 75 / 85 / 163 / 29 ✓, none dropped ✓.
No `templeNumber` feature projects inside 162.432–202.234 ✓. Minshuku Yasokubo **162.619 / 10 m**,
**0.187 km** in ✓, and no `accommodation` or `town` feature projects past it anywhere in the
section ✓. 162.619 → Sanuki-Aioi 187.287 = **24.668 km**, and it is the longest run with nothing
marked in the whole section, not merely on this day ✓. Station / shrine / shop at 187.287 /
187.849 / 188.347, span **1.060** → 1.1 ✓. 188.347 → the vending machine at 200.944 =
**12.597 km** with nothing between ✓. Bus stop at **202.158**, and 202.23351 − 202.158 =
**0.0755 km** → 76 m ✓. The uncited seventh feature is the toilet at **162.507**, which falls
*before* Yasokubo — so "After it 24.7 km carry nothing marked" is not compromised by it ✓.

- **The restraint reads as restraint, not as a hole.** Scanned: the narrative and reflection
  contain none of *return, circle, close, complete, finish, nirvana* — nor *climb, ascent, descent,
  pass, hard, flat, level, gentle, easy*, nor any date, across all eleven stages I reviewed. The
  day is given as two temples, a distance, and seven places in the order the line passes them, and
  the sequence *is* the day: 0.2 km, then 24.7 km of nothing, then three things inside 1.1 km,
  then 12.6 km with one vending machine, then a bus stop 76 m short. A reader who has walked 39.8
  km of that does not need the text to name what it was. **The one thing that would have made it a
  hole — saying nothing at all about the shape — is not what the drafter did.**
- **The reflection is within precedent.** "Every other section ends where the next one begins; this
  one ends where the first one began" is arithmetic on four `overview` blocks, and I checked all
  four: awa `[134.503,34.16,15]` → `[134.528,33.732]`, tosa `[134.528,33.732]` →
  `[132.774,32.961]`, iyo `[132.774,32.961]` → `[133.587,33.968]`, sanuki `[133.587,33.968]` →
  `[134.503,34.16,15]`. Every join is literal and the last one closes on Awa's first. "Who decides
  when a walk is finished?" asserts nothing, exactly as Awa's stage 4 and Tosa's stage 14 assert
  nothing. Allowed.
- **The 霊山寺前 translation is allowed, and it is not as new a liberty as the drafter feared.**
  "named for the temple it stands in front of" renders 霊山寺 (the `ja` name `overview.endPoint`
  itself gives the temple, checked) plus 前, "in front of". Rendering Japanese names into English
  is already established across the corpus, not invented here: Awa writes 日和佐八幡神社 as "Hiwasa
  Hachiman Shrine" — 神社 translated, not romanised — and Tosa writes 熊野神社 as "Kumano Shrine"
  and 妙見宮 as "Myōken-gū". Translating 前 is the same operation on a different morpheme. The
  clause adds **nothing** the tag does not contain, and the spatial relation the tag asserts is
  independently corroborated by the 76 m measurement. Two further points in its favour: it is
  *less* assertive than quoting the raw string, since it does not require the reader to read
  kanji; and the same node, or its twin, stands at 0.075 km on Awa's stage 0 under the identical
  name, so the corpus will meet this string again. **Allow it for the pilgrimage.**
- **The forward look stands** on the ruling recorded at Awa's stage 1, and rests on two committed
  `overview` fields holding the same literal triple.
- **Refusing the `end.note`'s own "closing return" was right.** The note is committed data and
  §6's second sentence would admit it, but *closing* is a reading of what the circuit means, not a
  property of a 39.802 km line between two temples — the same test that refused "nirvana" and the
  911 m superlative. The narrative describes the stretch; it does not tell the walker what it is.

**Edited in Task 8c. The closing return, and what it still does not say.**

Thirteen figures to nine, five pieces of dataset vocabulary to none, and **the refusal Task 7a read hardest
is intact**: this narrative gained none of *return*, *circle*, *close*, *complete*, *finish* or *nirvana*,
and none of *climb*, *ascent*, *descent*, *pass*, *hard*, *flat*, *level*, *gentle* or *easy*. It still
gives the day as two temples, a distance, and what is mapped between them in the order the line passes it,
and it still does not tell the walker what the day means.

**Two strings from that list were already in the shipped text and are left exactly as their drafter wrote
them**, recorded here so a later reader is not surprised to find them: "Ryōzen-ji **closes** Sanuki", which
is the section-boundary formula Awa's stage 4 and Iyo's stage 13 use in the same words and which closes a
*section*, not a circuit; and the reflection's "Who decides when a walk is **finished**?", which Task 7a
cleared by name as asserting nothing. Task 7a's scan was of the day for the *reading* of the circuit rather
than of the text for the strings, and it was right that the reading is absent. This pass introduced neither
word and removed neither.

```
theme:      From the eighty-eighth to the first
narrative:  A temple stands at each end of this day and none between them: Ōkubo-ji, the eighty-eighth, and Ryōzen-ji, the first. 39.8 km, the second-longest of Sanuki's days, and fewer places are mapped on it than on any other day here. Minshuku Yasokubo comes 0.2 km in, and past Ōkubo-ji nothing else is mapped as a place to stay or a town. After it 24.7 km carry nothing mapped at all, the longest such stretch in this section. Then a station, a shrine and a shop inside 1.1 km, then 12.6 km more with a single vending machine on them. The last thing mapped is a bus stop 76 m short of the end, named for the temple it stands in front of. Ryōzen-ji closes Sanuki, and Awa's first day starts from the same point.
reflection: Every other section ends where the next one begins; this one ends where the first one began. Who decides when a walk is finished?
```

- **"Minshuku Yasokubo comes 0.2 km in and is the only place to stay, and the only named accommodation or
  town, the record holds anywhere past Ōkubo-ji" → "Minshuku Yasokubo comes 0.2 km in, and past Ōkubo-ji
  nothing else is mapped as a place to stay or a town".** *The record holds* is out-list vocabulary, and
  *named accommodation or town* is the day cut's own term for its fallback candidates, which no walker has a
  definition for. The replacement is Awa's stage 3 construction and says **both** things the original said —
  Yasokubo is the only lodging past the temple, and there is no town either — with the hedge in the verb,
  where it is owed: this is an absence, and an unmapped bed would still be a bed. **No `type: accommodation`
  or `type: town` feature projects past 162.432 km**, unchanged. *Place to stay* is used and not *bed*,
  because this section deliberately uses neither "gate" nor "bed" and that abstention is Task 7a's to keep,
  not this pass's to spend.
- **"seven places recorded on it, the fewest of the six" → "fewer places are mapped on it than on any other
  day here".** *Recorded* goes; the comparison is over six days counted the same way out of one file, and it
  keeps its bound in *here*. Two figures out, and the seven places are still enumerated by the four
  sentences that follow — the lodging, the blank, the station, the shrine, the shop, the vending machine and
  the bus stop.
- **"After it 24.7 km carry nothing marked at all" → "… nothing mapped at all"**, and **"The last thing the
  record marks is a bus stop 76 m short of the end" → "The last thing mapped is a bus stop 76 m short of the
  end".** Two words. *Last* asserts none after it, so the hedge is load-bearing in both and is kept in the
  verb. **24.668 km and 0.0755 km are unchanged**, and so is the superlative's bound — "the longest such
  stretch in this section".
- **"named for the temple it stands in front of" is carried word for word.** It is the translation of
  霊山寺前 that Task 7a examined at length and allowed for the pilgrimage; rewording it would detach that
  ruling, and it adds nothing the node's own name does not contain.
- **"Ryōzen-ji closes Sanuki at 202.2 km" → "Ryōzen-ji closes Sanuki".** The section's whole length is a
  cumulative kilometre by definition. The sentence is now word for word the form Awa's stage 4 and Iyo's
  stage 13 close on, which is the point: this section ends the way the other three do, and the fact that its
  next point is Awa's first is left to the reflection, where Task 7a checked it against all four `overview`
  blocks.
- **"the second-longest of Sanuki's six days" → "the second-longest of Sanuki's days".** One figure; the
  rank and its bound are unchanged.
- **The reflection is untouched.** This is the day the section preamble says a reviewer should read with one
  test — "is any sentence here claiming something the files do not hold" — and the answer is still no. **The
  narrative stays at nine figures and that is not an editing failure**: this day *is* a sequence of measured
  blanks, exactly as Tosa's stage 11 is, and 0.2 km, 24.7 km, 1.1 km, 12.6 km and 76 m in that order are the
  whole of what the files hold about it. Take any of them out and the day loses its shape.

**Read in Task 9b. The edit stands; the flag is cleared, and the two pre-existing words are right to
stand.**

**The ruling on the closing return.** Task 8c introduced none of *return, circle, close, complete, finish*
or *nirvana* into this narrative and removed neither of the two already in it. Both calls are correct.
**"Ryōzen-ji closes Sanuki" must stay.** It is the section-boundary formula, word for word what Awa's stage
4 and Iyo's stage 13 say of their own ends, and what it closes is a *section*, not a circuit. Removing it
would break a shape all four sections hold, and would break it on the one day where a reader would most
notice this section behaving unlike the others — which is the opposite of what the refusal is for, since the
refusal exists to stop this day being made to mean *more* than the other thirty-nine, not to make it mean
less. **"Who decides when a walk is finished?" must stay too.** Task 7a cleared it by name; it asks who
decides rather than asserting that anything is finished, and it is the same form as Awa's stage 4 and Tosa's
stage 14 closings. **What the refusal is actually for is the reading of the circuit, and no sentence here
makes it**: *return*, *circle* and *nirvana* appear nowhere in the six narratives, themes or reflections,
scanned on the committed `stages.json`, and the day is still two temples, a distance, and what is mapped
between them in the order the line passes it.

- **"Minshuku Yasokubo … is the only place to stay, and the only named accommodation or town, the record
  holds anywhere past Ōkubo-ji" to "Minshuku Yasokubo comes 0.2 km in, and past Ōkubo-ji nothing else is
  mapped as a place to stay or a town" says both things the drafted sentence said**, with the hedge in the
  verb where an absence owes one, and drops *named accommodation or town* — the day cut's own term for its
  fallback candidates, which no walker has a definition for. No `type: accommodation` or `type: town`
  feature projects past 162.432 km, unchanged.
- **"place to stay" and not "bed", and no "gate" anywhere.** The section's abstention from both words is
  Task 7a's to keep and this pass did not spend it; scanned across all six, neither word appears.
- **"the longest such stretch in this section" keeps its bound**, 24.668 km and the 76 m are unchanged, and
  **"named for the temple it stands in front of" is carried word for word**, so Task 7a's ruling allowing
  the 霊山寺前 translation for the pilgrimage still attaches to the clause it examined.
- **"fewer places are mapped on it than on any other day here" is verified** at 7 features carrying
  `stageIndex: 5` against 30, 75, 85, 163 and 29, none dropped — and the seven are still enumerated one by
  one by the four sentences that follow, so the count's loss costs the day none of its shape.
